I need to find the limit as x approaches infinity of (3x² + 2x + 1)/(5x² + 4x + 2).

</think>

To find the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the function 

\[
\frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2},
\]

follow these steps:

**Step 1: Identify the highest power of \( x \)**

Both the numerator and the denominator are polynomials where the highest power of \( x \) is \( x^2 \).

**Step 2: Divide each term by the highest power**

Divide every term in both the numerator and the denominator by \( x^2 \):

\[
\frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \frac{\frac{3x^2}{x^2} + \frac{2x}{x^2} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{\frac{5x^2}{x^2} + \frac{4x}{x^2} + \frac{2}{x^2}} = \frac{3 + \frac{2}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}}{5 + \frac{4}{x} + \frac{2}{x^2}}.
\]

**Step 3: Evaluate the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity**

As \( x \) approaches infinity, the terms with \( \frac{1}{x} \) and \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) approach zero:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(3 + \frac{2}{x} + \frac{1}{x^2}\right) = 3,
\]
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(5 + \frac{4}{x} + \frac{2}{x^2}\right) = 5.
\]

**Step 4: Compute the final limit**

Putting it all together:

\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \frac{3x^2 + 2x + 1}{5x^2 + 4x + 2} = \frac{3}{5}.
\]

**Final Answer:** 

The limit is \(\boxed{\dfrac{3}{5}}\).

To determine the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} \), I first consider the degrees of the polynomials in the numerator and denominator. Both have a degree of 2.

Next, I identify the leading coefficients: for the numerator, it's 1 (from \( x^2 \)), and for the denominator, also 1 (from \( x^2 \)). Since both the highest degree terms have the same coefficient and the degrees are equal, the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity is simply the ratio of these leading coefficients.

Thus, the limit is \( \frac{1}{1} = 1 \).
</think>

To determine the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of the function:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1}
\]

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Analyze the Degrees of the Polynomials:**
   - The numerator is \( x^2 + x + 1 \), which has a degree of 2.
   - The denominator is \( x^2 - x + 1 \), which also has a degree of 2.

2. **Compare Leading Coefficients:**
   - The leading term in the numerator is \( x^2 \) with a coefficient of 1.
   - The leading term in the denominator is \( x^2 \) with a coefficient of 1.

3. **Determine the Limit:**
   - When both the numerator and denominator have the same degree, the limit as \( x \) approaches infinity is the ratio of their leading coefficients.
   - Therefore:

\[
\lim_{{x \to \infty}} \frac{x^2 + x + 1}{x^2 - x + 1} = \frac{1}{1} = 1
\]

The limit as \( x \) approaches infinity of \( (1 + \frac{2}{x})^x \) can be evaluated using the standard result from calculus. 

We know that:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{a}{x}\right)^x = e^a
\]
Here, \( a = 2 \). Therefore, substituting this value into the formula gives:
\[
\lim_{x \to \infty} \left(1 + \frac{2}{x}\right)^x = e^2
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{e^2}
\]

Okay, so I need to find the derivative of the function F(x) = 3x² - 5x + 4. Hmm, derivatives can be a bit tricky at first, but I think I remember some rules from my calculus class.

First, I recall that the derivative of a function gives you the slope of the tangent line at any point on the curve. That's pretty cool because it tells you how steep the function is changing at each point.

Now, looking at F(x), it's a polynomial, right? So each term can be differentiated separately using the power rule. The power rule says that if you have a term like ax^n, its derivative is n*ax^(n-1). Let me write that down to make sure I remember: d/dx [ax^n] = a*n*x^(n-1).

Okay, so let's break down F(x) into each term:

1. The first term is 3x².
2. The second term is -5x.
3. The third term is +4.

I'll differentiate each one by one.

Starting with the first term: 3x². Applying the power rule, I bring down the exponent (which is 2) as a coefficient, multiply it by the existing coefficient (3), so that's 3*2 = 6. Then, reduce the exponent by 1, which gives me x^(2-1) = x^1 or just x. So the derivative of 3x² should be 6x.

Next term: -5x. This is a linear term because the exponent on x is 1. Applying the power rule again, bring down the exponent (which is 1), multiply by the coefficient (-5), giving me -5*1 = -5. Then reduce the exponent by 1, so x^(1-1) = x^0, which is just 1. So this term simplifies to -5.

Wait a second, but what's the derivative of a constant? I remember that the slope of a horizontal line (which is what a constant function is) doesn't change, so its derivative should be zero. So for the third term, +4, when we take the derivative, it should just disappear because 4 is like 4x^0, and applying the power rule gives us 0*4x^-1 = 0.

Putting it all together, the derivatives of each term are:

- First term: 6x
- Second term: -5
- Third term: 0

Adding them up, F'(x) = 6x -5 + 0. The last term is just gone because its derivative is zero. So simplifying that, it's 6x -5.

Wait, let me double-check to make sure I didn't miss anything. Did I handle the coefficients correctly? Yes: for x², multiplied by 2 gives 6; for x, multiplied by 1 gives -5. The constant term disappears because its derivative is zero. So yeah, that seems right.
</think>

The derivative of F(x) = 3x² - 5x + 4 can be found using the power rule:

- The derivative of 3x² is 6x (bring down the exponent as a coefficient and reduce it by one).
- The derivative of -5x is -5 (since x¹ becomes x⁰, which is 1, multiplied by -5 gives -5).
- The derivative of the constant term +4 is 0.

The derivative of \( \frac{1}{x} \) is calculated using either the power rule or the limit definition. 

**Using the Power Rule:**
\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^{-1}) = -1 \cdot x^{-2} = -\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

**Verification with Limit Definition:**
\[
f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\frac{1}{x+h} - \frac{1}{x}}{h} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-h}{hx(x+h)} = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{-1}{x(x+h)} = -\frac{1}{x^2}
\]

Both methods confirm that the derivative is \( -\frac{1}{x^2} \).

Step 3: Calculate derivatives of numerator and denominator:
- d/dx(sin(x)) = cos(x)
- d/dx(cos(x)) = -sin(x)

Step 4: Substitute into the quotient rule formula:
f'(x) = [cos(x)*cos(x) - sin(x)*(-sin(x))] / [cos(x)]²

Step 5: Simplify using the Pythagorean identity (sin²(x) + cos²(x) = 1):
f'(x) = 1 / cos²(x) = sec²(x)

Okay, now I have a simpler equation: ln(y) equals x times the natural log of x. The next step is to differentiate both sides with respect to x. On the left side, since y is a function of x, its derivative will involve dy/dx multiplied by the derivative of ln(y), which is 1/y. So,

= ln(x) + (x*(1/x))

dy/dx = x^x * (ln(x) + 1)

1. Let \( y = x^x \).

   - Left side: \( \frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} \).

\[
\left( \frac{f}{g} \right)' = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}
\]

Here, \( f(x) = 1 \) and \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \).

   \]

3. **Simplify the expression:**

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{\ln x}{x^2} \), we will use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \), its derivative is:

\[
h'(x) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}
\]

**Step 1:** Identify \( f(x) \) and \( g(x) \).

- \( f(x) = \ln x \)
- \( g(x) = x^2 \)

- \( f'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)
- \( g'(x) = 2x \)

\[
F'(x) = \frac{\left(\frac{1}{x}\right)(x^2) - (\ln x)(2x)}{(x^2)^2}
\]

**Step 4:** Simplify the numerator and denominator.

- Numerator:
  \[

  \]
  
- Denominator:
  \[
  (x^2)^2 = x^4
  \]

\[

\]

\[
\sec^2(y) \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = 1
\]

Solving for \(\frac{dy}{dx}\):

\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = \frac{1}{\sec^2(y)}
\]

Using the identity \(\tan^2 y + 1 = \sec^2 y\) and substituting \(\tan y = x\):

\[
\sec^2(y) = x^2 + 1
\]

Thus,

\[

\]

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the product rule. The product rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = f(x)g(x) \), then its derivative is:

\]

In our case:

The function F(x) is given as \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \). To find its second derivative, we proceed as follows:

**First Derivative:**
Using the chain rule:
\[
F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} \left( e^{x^2} \right) = e^{x^2} \cdot 2x = 2x e^{x^2}
\]

**Second Derivative:**
Applying the product rule to \( F'(x) = 2x e^{x^2} \):
\[
F''(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (2x e^{x^2}) = 2 e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot \frac{d}{dx}(e^{x^2})
\]
Again, using the chain rule for \( \frac{d}{dx}(e^{x^2}) \):
\[
F''(x) = 2 e^{x^2} + 2x \cdot 2x e^{x^2} = 2 e^{x^2} + 4x^2 e^{x^2}
\]
Factor out \( 2 e^{x^2} \):
\[
F''(x) = 2 e^{x^2} (1 + 2x^2)
\]

**Final Answer:**
The second derivative of \( F(x) = e^{x^2} \) is:
\[
F''(x) = 2e^{x^2}(1 + 2x^2)
\]
In a more compact form, it's often written as:
\[
F''(x) = (4x^2 + 2)e^{x^2}
\]

The function F(x) is given as:

\[ F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \]

To find its derivative, we use the quotient rule. 

The quotient rule states that if \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), then:
\[ f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} \]

Here, let:
- \( u(x) = x \)
- \( v(x) = 1 + x^2 \)

First, compute the derivatives:
- \( u'(x) = 1 \)
- \( v'(x) = 2x \)

Applying the quotient rule:
\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2} \\
= \frac{(1)(1 + x^2) - (x)(2x)}{(1 + x^2)^2} \\
= \frac{1 + x^2 - 2x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2} \\
= \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2}
\]

So, the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{x}{1 + x^2} \) is:

\[ F'(x) = \frac{1 - x^2}{(1 + x^2)^2} \]

The limit of \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side is evaluated by observing the behavior of the function near zero.

As \( x \) becomes very small (approaching 0), \( x^2 \) also becomes very small, causing \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) to increase without bound. Therefore, the limit approaches infinity.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Problem:** We need to find \( \lim_{{x \to 0^+}} \frac{1}{x^2} \).

2. **Analyze the Behavior Near Zero:**
   - As \( x \) approaches 0 from the positive side, \( x \) is a small positive number.
   - Squaring \( x \) (\( x^2 \)) results in an even smaller positive number.

3. **Evaluate the Denominator:**
   - Since \( x^2 \) is approaching 0, it becomes very small.

4. **Determine the Overall Behavior:**
   - Dividing 1 by a very small positive number (approaching zero) results in a value that grows without bound towards infinity.

5. **Conclusion:**
   - The limit of \( \frac{1}{x^2} \) as \( x \) approaches 0 from the right is positive infinity.

**Final Answer:**  
The limit tends to positive infinity, so it does not have a finite value.

   The derivative of \( x^n \) is \( n \cdot x^{n-1} \).

The derivative of \( f(x) = \sin(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \cos(x) \).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **Understand the Problem**: We are tasked with finding the derivative of the function \( f(x) = \sin(x) \). This involves applying basic differentiation rules for trigonometric functions.

2. **Recall Differentiation Rules**: The fundamental rule for differentiating sine is a key part of calculus. Specifically, the derivative of \( \sin(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is known to be \( \cos(x) \).

3. **Apply the Rule**:
   - Start with \( f(x) = \sin(x) \).
   - Differentiate both sides with respect to \( x \):
     \[
     f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}[\sin(x)] = \cos(x)
     \]
   
4. **Verify Using Limits (Optional)**:
   - For confirmation, use the limit definition of the derivative:
     \[
     f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin(x + h) - \sin(x)}{h}
     \]
   - Use the trigonometric identity \( \sin(a + b) = \sin(a)\cos(b) + \cos(a)\sin(b) \):
     \[
     f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin(x)\cos(h) + \cos(x)\sin(h) - \sin(x)}{h}
     \]
   - Simplify the expression by factoring out \( \sin(x) \):
     \[
     f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \left[ \sin(x) \cdot \frac{\cos(h) - 1}{h} + \cos(x) \cdot \frac{\sin(h)}{h} \right]
     \]
   - Knowing that \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{\sin(h)}{h} = 1 \) and \( \lim_{h \to 0} (\cos(h) - 1)/h = 0 \), the expression simplifies to:
     \[
     f'(x) = 0 + \cos(x) \cdot 1 = \cos(x)
     \]

5. **Conclusion**: The derivative of \( \sin(x) \) is indeed \( \cos(x) \).

**Final Answer:**
The derivative of \( f(x) = \sin(x) \) is \( f'(x) = \cos(x) \).

   f'(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^{x+h} - e^x}{h}
   \]
3. Simplify using properties of exponents: \( e^{x+h} = e^x \cdot e^h \).
4. Substitute and factor out \( e^x \):
   \[
   f'(x) = e^x \cdot \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h}
   \]
5. Recognize that the limit \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h} = 1 \).
6. Therefore, \( f'(x) = e^x \cdot 1 = e^x \).

**Answer:**  
The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is \( e^x \).

The derivative of \( \tan(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is \( \sec^2(x) \).

Step-by-Step Explanation:
1. Recognize that \( \tan(x) = \frac{\sin(x)}{\cos(x)} \).
2. Apply the quotient rule: 
   - Let \( g(x) = \sin(x) \), so \( g'(x) = \cos(x) \).
   - Let \( h(x) = \cos(x) \), so \( h'(x) = -\sin(x) \).
3. Use the formula:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{g'(x)h(x) - g(x)h'(x)}{[h(x)]^2}
   \]
4. Substitute and simplify:
   \[
   f'(x) = \frac{\cos(x)\cos(x) - \sin(x)(-\sin(x))}{\cos^2(x)} = \frac{\cos^2(x) + \sin^2(x)}{\cos^2(x)} = \frac{1}{\cos^2(x)} = \sec^2(x)
   \]

The derivative of \( y = x^x \) with respect to \( x \) is calculated by first taking the natural logarithm of both sides:

\[
\ln(y) = x \cdot \ln(x)
\]

Differentiating implicitly with respect to \( x \):

\[
\frac{1}{y} \cdot \frac{dy}{dx} = \ln(x) + 1
\]

Multiplying both sides by \( y \):

\[
\frac{dy}{dx} = y (\ln(x) + 1)
\]

Substituting back \( y = x^x \):

\[
\frac{d}{dx}(x^x) = x^x (\ln(x) + 1)
\]

The derivative of F(x) = \(\frac{\ln x}{x^2}\) is calculated using the product rule after rewriting it as a product of two functions. 

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

   \(F(x) = (\ln x) \cdot x^{-2}\)

2. **Apply Product Rule:**  
   If \(u = \ln x\) and \(v = x^{-2}\), then:
   - \(u' = \frac{1}{x}\)
   - \(v' = -2x^{-3}\)

   Using the product rule, \(F'(x) = u'v + uv'\).

3. **Compute Each Term:**  
   - First term: \(u'v = \frac{1}{x} \cdot x^{-2} = x^{-3}\)
   - Second term: \(uv' = \ln x \cdot (-2)x^{-3} = -\frac{2 \ln x}{x^3}\)

The second derivative of \( F(x) = x^3 \ln x \) is found by first taking the first derivative using the product rule, then differentiating again.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x))**:
   - Let \( u = x^3 \) and \( v = \ln x \).
   - Using the product rule: \( F'(x) = u'v + uv' \).
   - Compute \( u' = 3x^2 \) and \( v' = \frac{1}{x} \).
   - Therefore, \( F'(x) = 3x^2 \ln x + x^3 \cdot \frac{1}{x} = 3x^2 \ln x + x^2 \).

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x))**:
   - Differentiate \( F'(x) \):
     - For \( 3x^2 \ln x \), apply the product rule again.
       - Let \( a = 3x^2 \) and \( b = \ln x \).
       - Compute \( a' = 6x \) and \( b' = \frac{1}{x} \).
       - Result: \( 6x \ln x + 3x \).
     - For \( x^2 \), the derivative is \( 2x \).

   - Combine these results:
     - \( F''(x) = (6x \ln x + 3x) + 2x = 6x \ln x + 5x \).

3. **Final Answer**:
   - Factor out \( x \): \( F''(x) = x(6 \ln x + 5) \).

**Answer:**  
{Final Answer: "x*(6 ln(x) + 5)"}

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **Product Rule**. The Product Rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = f(x) \cdot g(x) \), its derivative \( h'(x) \) is given by:

\[
h'(x) = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
\]

**Applying the Product Rule to \( F(x) = x \ln x \):**

1. **Identify the two functions:**
   - Let \( f(x) = x \)
   - Let \( g(x) = \ln x \)

2. **Find the derivatives of each function:**
   - \( f'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (x) = 1 \)
   - \( g'(x) = \frac{d}{dx} (\ln x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

3. **Apply the Product Rule formula:**
   \[
   F'(x) = f'(x) \cdot g(x) + f(x) \cdot g'(x)
   \]
   Substituting the derivatives:
   \[
   F'(x) = 1 \cdot \ln x + x \cdot \left( \frac{1}{x} \right )
   \]

4. **Simplify the expression:**
   \[
   F'(x) = \ln x + 1
   \]

The problem requires finding the derivative of the function \( \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \). To solve this, we apply the quotient rule from calculus. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( \frac{f(x)}{g(x)} \), its derivative is given by:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{f(x)}{g(x)}\right) = \frac{f'(x)g(x) - f(x)g'(x)}{[g(x)]^2}
\]

Here, \( f(x) = \sin x \) and \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \). 

First, find the derivatives of \( f(x) \) and \( g(x) \):
- The derivative of \( f(x) = \sin x \) is \( f'(x) = \cos x \).
- The derivative of \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \) is \( g'(x) = 2x \).

Substitute these into the quotient rule formula:

\[
\frac{d}{dx}\left(\frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1}\right) = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x \cdot 2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

Thus, the derivative of \( \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \) is:

\[
\boxed{\frac{(x^2 + 1)\cos x - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}
\]

The second derivative of F(x) = e^{x²} is calculated by first finding the first derivative using the chain rule, resulting in F’(x) = 2x e^{x²}. Applying the product rule to this result gives the second derivative as F''(x) = 2e^{x²}(1 + 2x²).

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x))**: 
   - Start with F(x) = e^{x²}.
   - Let u = x², so du/dx = 2x.
   - Apply the chain rule: d/dx(e^u) = e^u * du/dx = e^{x²} * 2x.
   - Therefore, F'(x) = 2x e^{x²}.

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x))**:
   - Now differentiate F'(x) = 2x e^{x²}.
   - Use the product rule: d/dx(uv) = u'v + uv', where u = 2x and v = e^{x²}.
   - Compute u': du/dx = 2.
   - Compute v': dv/dx = derivative of e^{x²} is e^{x²} * 2x (using chain rule again).
   - Apply the product rule: F''(x) = (2)(e^{x²}) + (2x)(2x e^{x²}).

3. **Simplify**:
   - Expand terms: F''(x) = 2 e^{x²} + 4x² e^{x²}.
   - Factor out common term 2 e^{x²}: F''(x) = 2 e^{x²}(1 + 2x²).

**Final Answer:** {Final Answer: "2 e^{x^2} (1 + 2 x^2)}"}

- \( u' = 1 \)
- \( v' = -2x(1 + x^2)^{-2} \)

\]

The problem requires finding the derivative with respect to \( x \) of \( \sec^2(x) \).

To solve this:
1. Recognize that \( f(x) = \sec^2(x) \).
2. Apply the chain rule: Let \( u = \sec(x) \), then \( f(u) = u^2 \).
3. Compute the derivative of \( f(u) \) with respect to \( x \): 
   \[
   \frac{df}{dx} = 2u \cdot \frac{du}{dx}
   \]
4. Substitute back:
   \[
   \frac{df}{dx} = 2\sec(x) \cdot \sec(x)\tan(x) = 2\sec^2(x)\tan(x)
   \]

   - Recall that lim_{x→0} (sin x)/x = 1 is a well-known standard limit derived from geometric principles involving the unit circle and sector areas.

To find the derivative of the function \( F(x) = 3x^2 - 5x + 4 \), we will apply the basic rules of differentiation, specifically the power rule.

1. **Differentiate each term separately:**
   
   - For the term \( 3x^2 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(3x^2) = 2 \times 3x^{2-1} = 6x
     \]
     
   - For the term \( -5x \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(-5x) = -5
     \]
     
   - For the constant term \( 4 \):
     \[
     \frac{d}{dx}(4) = 0
     \]

2. **Combine the derivatives of each term:**
   
   \[
   F'(x) = 6x - 5 + 0 = 6x - 5
   \]

**Final Answer:**  
\boxed{F'(x) = 6x - 5}

The derivative of \( e^x \) with respect to \( x \) is computed as follows:

1. **Definition of Derivative**: The derivative of a function \( f(x) \) at \( x \) is given by:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} f(x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{f(x + h) - f(x)}{h}
   \]
2. **Apply to \( e^x \)**: Let \( f(x) = e^x \).
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^{x+h} - e^x}{h}
   \]
3. **Simplify**:
   \[
   = \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^x \cdot e^h - e^x}{h} = e^x \cdot \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h}
   \]
4. **Evaluate Limit**: Using the known limit \( \lim_{h \to 0} \frac{e^h - 1}{h} = 1 \),
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (e^x) = e^x \cdot 1 = e^x
   \]
   
**Answer**: {Final Answer: "e^x"}

- The derivative of \( x^3 \) is \( 3x^2 \).

- The derivative of \( x \) is \( 1 \).
- The derivative of the constant term \( 1 \) is \( 0 \).

To find the second derivative of the function \( F(x) = x^4 - 3x^3 + x \), we proceed as follows:

1. **First Derivative**:
   \[
   F'(x) = \frac{d}{dx}(x^4) - \frac{d}{dx}(3x^3) + \frac{d}{dx}(x)
   \]
   Applying the power rule:
   \[
   F'(x) = 4x^3 - 9x^2 + 1
   \]

   \[

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \cdot e^{x} \), we'll use the **product rule**. The product rule states that if you have a function which is the product of two functions, say \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \), then the derivative is:

\[
(uv)' = u'v + uv'
\]

In this case:
- Let \( u(x) = x \)
- Let \( v(x) = e^{x} \)

First, find the derivatives of \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):
\[

\]
\[
v'(x) = e^{x}
\]

\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]
\[

\]
\[
F'(x) = e^{x}(1 + x)

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = \frac{1}{x^2 + 1} \), I'll start by recognizing that this is a composite function. 

I can rewrite \( F(x) \) as \( (x^2 + 1)^{-1} \) to make applying the chain rule easier.

Next, I'll apply the chain rule: if we have a function \( f(g(x)) \), its derivative is \( f'(g(x)) \cdot g'(x) \).

Here, let \( g(x) = x^2 + 1 \) and \( f(u) = u^{-1} \). The derivative of \( f(u) \) with respect to \( u \) is \( -u^{-2} \), and the derivative of \( g(x) \) with respect to \( x \) is \( 2x \).

Multiplying these derivatives together gives \( F'(x) = - (x^2 + 1)^{-2} \cdot 2x \).

Finally, simplifying this expression yields \( F'(x) = -\frac{2x}{(x^2 + 1)^2} \).

To find the derivative of \( E^{x^2} \) with respect to \( x \), we'll use the chain rule. Here's a step-by-step explanation:

1. **Identify the outer and inner functions**:
   - Outer function: \( E^u \)
   - Inner function: \( u = x^2 \)

2. **Differentiate the outer function with respect to the inner function**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{du} (E^u) = E^u
   \]

3. **Differentiate the inner function with respect to \( x \)**:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (x^2) = 2x
   \]

4. **Apply the chain rule** by multiplying the derivatives from steps 2 and 3:
   \[
   \frac{d}{dx} (E^{x^2}) = E^{x^2} \times 2x = 2x E^{x^2}
   \]

The derivative of the function \( F(x) = \frac{e^x}{1 + e^x} \) is calculated using the quotient rule. Applying the quotient rule:

\[
F'(x) = \frac{(e^x)'(1 + e^x) - e^x(e^x)'}{(1 + e^x)^2} = \frac{e^x (1 + e^x) - e^{2x}}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]
Simplifying the numerator:

\[
e^x (1 + e^x) - e^{2x} = e^x + e^{2x} - e^{2x} = e^x
\]

Thus, 

\[
F'(x) = \frac{e^x}{(1 + e^x)^2}
\]

Answer: \( \boxed{\dfrac{e^{x}}{(1 + e^{x})^{2}}} \)

To find the derivative of \( F(x) = x \ln x \), we'll use the **Product Rule**. The Product Rule states that if you have a function \( h(x) = u(x)v(x) \), then the derivative \( h'(x) \) is:

\[
h'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x)
\]

In our case:
- Let \( u(x) = x \)  
  So, \( u'(x) = 1 \)

- Let \( v(x) = \ln x \)  
  So, \( v'(x) = \frac{1}{x} \)

Applying the Product Rule:

\[
F'(x) = u'(x)v(x) + u(x)v'(x) \\
F'(x) = (1)(\ln x) + (x)\left(\frac{1}{x}\right) \\
F'(x) = \ln x + 1
\]

**Final Answer:**
\[
\boxed{\ln x + 1}
\]

To find the derivative of \( f(x) = \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \), we'll use the **quotient rule**. The quotient rule states that if you have a function \( f(x) = \frac{u(x)}{v(x)} \), its derivative is:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{u'(x)v(x) - u(x)v'(x)}{[v(x)]^2}
\]

Let's identify \( u(x) \) and \( v(x) \):

- **Numerator (\( u(x) \))**: \( \sin x \)
  - Derivative: \( u'(x) = \cos x \)

- **Denominator (\( v(x) \))**: \( x^2 + 1 \)
  - Derivative: \( v'(x) = 2x \)

Now, apply the quotient rule:

\[
f'(x) = \frac{\cos x (x^2 + 1) - \sin x (2x)}{(x^2 + 1)^2}
\]

Simplify the numerator if possible. In this case, there's no further simplification needed.

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of \( \frac{\sin x}{x^2 + 1} \) with respect to \( x \) is:

\[
\boxed{\dfrac{(x^2 + 1)\cos x - 2x \sin x}{(x^2 + 1)^2}}
\]

The second derivative of F(x) = e^{x²} is calculated by first finding the first derivative using the chain rule, then applying the product rule to the result.

**Step-by-Step Explanation:**

1. **First Derivative (F'(x)):**
   - Let u = x².
   - Then, F(x) = e^u.
   - Using the chain rule: d/dx (e^u) = e^u * du/dx.
   - du/dx = 2x.
   - Therefore, F'(x) = e^{x²} * 2x.

2. **Second Derivative (F''(x)):**
   - Now, differentiate F'(x) = 2x * e^{x²}.
   - Let u = 2x and v = e^{x²}.
   - Using the product rule: d/dx (uv) = u'v + uv'.
     - u' = d/dx (2x) = 2.
     - v' = derivative of e^{x²} = e^{x²} * 2x.
   - Therefore, F''(x) = 2 * e^{x²} + 2x * (2x e^{x²}).
   - Simplify: F''(x) = 2e^{x²} + 4x² e^{x²}.
   - Factor out 2e^{x²}: F''(x) = 2(1 + 2x²)e^{x²}.

**Final Answer:**  
The second derivative of F(x) is {Final Answer: "2(1 + 2x²)e^{x²}"}

The derivative of sec(2x) with respect to x is found using the chain rule. First, let u = 2x. The derivative of sec(u) is sec(u)tan(u), and the derivative of u (which is 2x) is 2. Multiplying these together gives the result.

2. **Apply the chain rule:**
   - Let u = 2x.
   - The derivative of sec(u) with respect to u is sec(u)tan(u).
   - The derivative of u (which is 2x) with respect to x is 2.
3. **Multiply the derivatives:** d/dx [sec(2x)] = sec(2x) * tan(2x) * 2.

**Final Answer:**

The derivative of sec(2x) with respect to x is 2sec(2x)tan(2x).
